RAJYA SABHA 
Thursday, the 5th February, 2026 / 16 Magha, 1947 (Saka) 
The House met at eleven of the clock, 
MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
OBITUARY REFERENCE 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Hon. Members, I refer with profound sorrow to the passing away of 
Shri Thomas Kuthiravatto m, a former Member of this House, on the 12th  of January, 
2026, at the age of 80 years. He served as  a Member of the Rajy a Sabha representing 
the State of Kerala, from April, 1985 to April, 1991. 
  Born on the 1st  of June, 1945, at Kallissery in Chengannur, Kerala, Shri Thomas 
Kuthiravattom began his public  life through student and youth movements and steadily 
rose to occupy important positions in Kerala’s political landscape. Known for his 
simplicity, commitment to public service, and concern for the poor and downtrodden, 
Shri Thomas Kuthiravattom was widely respect ed across political and social circles in 
Kerala. 
  In the passing away of Shri Thomas  Kuthiravattom, the country has lost a 
dedicated social worker and an able Parliamentarian. 
  I request Members to rise in their places and observe silence as a mark of 
respect to the memory of the departed. 
(Hon. Members then stood in silence for one minute) 
MR. CHAIRMAN: Secretary-General will convey to th e members of the bereaved family 
our sense of profound sorrow and deep sympathy. 
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now, Papers to be laid on the Table.

MATTERS RAISED WITH PERMISSION  
Demand for a separate Ministry for Other Backward Classes at the Centre 
SHRI RYAGA KRISHNAIAH (Andhra Pradesh) :  Mr. Chairman, Sir, there is no 
separate Ministry or Department at the Cent ral level for the development and welfare of 
the OBCs, who constitute 56 per cent of the population in the  
country.  …(Interruptions)… There are 58 Central Ministries and 93 Departments of the 
Central Government and also there are separate ministries for many social communities 
like those for SCs, STs, minorities and women. …(Interruptions)…    
MR. CHAIRMAN:  This is not good. What you are doing is not  
democracy. …(Interruptions)…  
SHRI RYAGA KRISHNAIAH: There are separate ministerial departments recently 
established like the Ministries of Fisheries,  Skill Development and Cooperation, but not 
establishing a separate ministry for the deve lopment of BCs who constitute 56 per cent 
6
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
of the population is not justifiable.  At the Central level, 27 per cent reservation is being 
implemented in employment and education for BCs.  …(Interruptions)… The National 
Commission for Backward Classes and the National Backward Classes Finance and 
Development Corporation exist.  A Committ ee on Welfare of Other Backward Classes 
was also formed.  There have been a number of legal disputes on BC reservation and 
related issues.  A number of problems re garding the BC’s rese rvations are arising 
frequently.  Many schemes and projects intended for the development of BCs require 
supervision for their proper implementation , such as the Post Metric Scholarships 
Subsidy Loan Schemes. …(Interruptions)…  At present, many States implement various 
schemes and programs intended for the deve lopment of BCs.  Hence, they require 
supervision for their proper implementation.  At this juncture, it is imperative to 
establish a separate ministerial department for the development of  these communities.  
This only would facilitate special attention for the development of these communities.  
…(Interruptions)… So, a separate Ministry for BCs should be provided at the Central 
Government level and special attention paid  for the all-round development of OBCs, 
more particularly, in the education, econo mic and employment sectors to attain the 
goal of Viksit Bharat . …(Interruptions)… All the State Governments have separate 
ministries for BCs to monitor and coordinate activities. A separate Ministry for BCs at 
the Central level is also needed.  Many pr oblems pertaining to BC reservation are 
coming to light nowadays.  …(Interruptions)… Under these circumstances, a separate 
Ministry at the Union level should be consti tuted for the development and upliftment of 
BCs for faster development with special atte ntion which is overdue for the last several 
decades.                                                                                          
MR. CHAIRMAN:  The following hon. Member s associated themselves with the matter 
raised by hon. Member, Shri Ryaga Krishnaiah:  Dr. Sasmit Patra (Odisha),             
Shri Madan Rathore (Rajasthan), Dr. Bhim Singh (Bihar), Shri Shambhu Sharan Patel 
(Bihar), Shri Niranjan Bishi (Odisha), Shri  Surendra Singh Nagar (Uttar Pradesh) and 
Shri Babaubhai Jesanbhai Desai (Gujarat). …(Interruptions)…

12.00 Noon 
    ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
        Pendency of cases 
61. SHRI RAJEEV SHUKLA:  Will the Minist er of LAW AND JUSTICE be pleased to
state: 
(a) whether the Ministry is aware that the problem of  pend ency is particularly
acute at the lower levels of judiciary where the majority of cases are filed and
where the shortage of judges is most severe;
[ 05 February, 2026 ]
25

 
 
(b)     if so, whether any steps have been taken to address this issue; and 
(c)     if so, the details thereof and if not, the reasons therefor? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE OF THE MINISTRY OF LAW AND JUSTICE (SHRI ARJUN 
RAM MEGHWAL): (a) to(c) A statement is laid on the Table of the House.                                
                                                              Statement 
(a) to (c) Pendency of cases in courts arise due to several factors which inter 
alia, include complexity of the facts involv ed, nature of evidence, co-operation of 
stakeholders, viz., bar, investigation agenci es, witness and litigants, the availability of 
physical infrastructure, supporting court staff, etc. besides the shortage of judges. The 
disposal of cases is within the exclusive do main of the judiciary. However, the Central 
Government is committed for speedy disposal  of cases and reducing pendency as 
mandated under Article 21 of the Constitu tion and has taken several initiatives to 
provide an ecosystem for faster disposal of cases by the judiciary. 
i. The Government has been filling up vacanci es of Judges in the Supreme Court of 
India and the High Courts from time to time. From 01.05.2014 to 19.01.2026, 72 
Judges have been appointed in the Supreme Court. 1162 new Judges were 
appointed and 820 Additional Judges were  made permanent in the High Courts 
during the same period. The sanctioned strength of Judges of the High Courts 
has increased from 906 in May, 2014 to 1122 till date.  Further, filling up of vacant 
positions of the judicial officers in Di strict and Subordinate courts is the 
responsibility of the High Courts and State Governments concerned. As per the 
Constitutional framework, in exercise of  powers conferred under the proviso to 
Article 309 read with Articles 233 and 234 of the Constitution, the respective State 
Government, in consultation with the High Court, frames the rules and regulations 
regarding the appointment and recruitmen t of Judicial Officers.  The Hon’ble 
Supreme Court vide order passed in Ja nuary 2007 in the Malik Mazhar Sultan 
case, has inter-alia stipulated certain ti melines, which are to be followed by the 
States and the respective High Courts fo r recruitment of judges in District and 
Subordinate Courts. 
ii. The National Mission for Justice Delivery and Legal Reforms was set up in August, 
2011, with the twin objectives of increasing access to justice by reducing delays in 
the system and enhancing accountability through structural changes and by 
setting performance standards and capacities. 
iii. Under the Centrally Sponsored Scheme for Development of Infrastructure 
26
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Facilities for District and Subordinate Courts, a sum of Rs. 12,461.28 crore has 
been released since the in ception of this Scheme in 1993-94. The number of 
Court halls has increased from 15,818 (as on 30.06.2014) to 22,683 (as on 
31.12.2025) and the number of residential units has increased from 10,211 (as on 
30.06.2014) to 20,029 (as on 31.12.2025), under this Scheme. 
iv. The Phase-III of the eCourts Project (2023-2027) approved on 13.09.2023 with an 
outlay of Rs.7,210 crore to make justi ce delivery progressively more robust, easy 
and accessible. As on November, 2025, 618.36 crores pages of court records 
have been digitized in the High Courts an d District Courts. More than 3.89 crore 
hearings have taken place through Vide o conferencing and live streaming is 
functional in 11 High Courts. The numbe r of eSewa Kendras (facilitation centres) 
has increased to 2059 across High Courts and District Courts. 
v. Arrears Committees have been set up in a ll 25 High Courts and the District Courts 
as well to clear cases pending for more than five years.   
vi. Fast Track Courts have been established for expeditious trial of specific 
categories of cases including heinous cr imes, cases involving women, children, 
persons with disabilities, individuals afflic ted with terminal illnesses, and property 
related cases pending for more than five years. As per information received from 
the High Courts, 876 FTCs are functional across 21 States/UTs as on 30.11.2025. 
Further, ten Special Courts are functional in nine (9) States/UTs for expeditious 
trial and disposal of criminal cases involving elected MPs / MLAs.  
vii. Under the Centrally Sponsored Scheme namely Fast Track Special Courts 
(FTSCs) Scheme, 774 Fast  Track Special Courts (FTSCs) including 398 
exclusive POCSO (ePOCSO) Courts are fu nctional across 29 States/UTs as on 
30.11.2025, for the expeditious disposal of pending cases of Rape and POCSO 
Act. 
viii. The Government has also amended The Negotiable Instruments (Amendment) 
Act, 2018, The Commercial Courts (Ame ndment) Act, 2018, The Specific Relief 
(Amendment) Act, 2018, The Arbitratio n and Conciliation (Amendment) Act, 
2019 and The Criminal Laws (Amendment ) Act, 2018 with a view to reduce 
pendency.  
ix. Alternate Dispute Resolution methods have been promoted. The Commercial 
Courts Act, 2015 was amended in August , 2018 making Pre-institution Mediation 
and Settlement (PIMS) mandatory in cas e of commercial disputes. Amendment 
to the Arbitration and Conciliation Act, 1996 has been made by the Arbitration and 
[ 05 February, 2026 ]
27

 
 
Conciliation (Amendment) Ac t 2015 for expediting the speedy resolution of 
disputes by prescribing timelines. 
x. Lok Adalat is an important Alternative Disputes Resolution Mechanism available to 
common people, where the disputes/ cases pending in the court of law or at pre-
litigation stage are settled/ compromised amicably.   Under The Legal Services 
Authorities (LSA) Act, 1987, an award made  by a Lok Adalat is  deemed to be a 
decree of a civil court and is final and binding on all parties and no appeal lies 
against it before any court. National Lok Adalats are organized simultaneously in 
all Taluks, Districts and High Courts on a pre-fixed date.       
xi. The Government launched the Tele-Law program in 2017, which provides an 
effective and reliable e-interface platform co nnecting the needy and 
disadvantaged sections seeking legal advi ce and consultation with panel lawyers 
via video conferencing, telephone and ch at facilities available at the Common 
Service Centres (CSCs) situated in Gr am Panchayats and through Tele-Law 
mobile App. Pro bono culture and pro bono  lawyering have been  institutionalized 
in the country. Pro Bono Panel of advoca tes has been established in 23 High 
Courts. Pro Bono Clubs have been starte d in 109 Law Schools to instill Pro Bono 
culture in budding lawyers.

Major Completed Projects: The project for providing 2G mobile connectivity in 
uncovered villages and along National Highways across Arunachal Pradesh, Assam,

iv) Air Connectivity: 
The Ministry of Civil Aviation (MoCA) la unched the Regional Connectivity Scheme 
(UDAN) on 21st October, 2016. UDAN is a market-driven initiative where the 
development of regional air connectivity routes  is determined by market forces. Airlines 
assess the demand and supply needs for speci fic routes and lead the process under 
the scheme. Airlines are selected through a transparent bidding process. 
The following airports and heliports have be en identified for development and operation 
of RCS flights in the NE states under the UDAN scheme:  
AIRPORTS  
Passighat, Tezu, Hollongi, Jorhat, Lilabari , Rupsi, Tezpur, Dimapur, Shillong and 
Pakyong 
HELIPORTS 
 Arunachal Pradesh : Ziro, Walong, Itanagar (Naharlagun), Tuting, Yinghiong, 
Daparizo, Miao &Vijaynagar 
 Assam : Geleki&Nagaon 
 Meghalaya: Tura &Shillong (H) 
 Manipur: Tamenglong, Jiribam, Thanlon, Parbung, Moreh&Churachandpur 
 Nagaland:Phek, Kohima, Zunheboto, Tuensang, Mokokchung 
[ 05 February, 2026 ]
37

 
 
 Sikkim: Gangtok East, Pelling West &Namchi South 
All the above-mentioned 10 airports in  the North-Eastern States have been 
operationalized under the UDAN scheme and are currently operational, except Pakyong 
Airport. 
In addition, two heliports, namely Jiri bam and Tamenglong, have also been 
operationalized under Non-Scheduled Operator Permit (NSOP) operations.

v)  Power:  
A number of Hydro Electric Pr ojects with a total insta lled capacity of 5796 MW have 
been taken up in NER. The major projects include Subansiri Lower (1750 MW), Dibang 
Multi-Purpose project (2880 MW), Teesta  Stage VI (500 MW) etc. Under the NER 
Power System Improvement Project for Si x States (Assam, Manipur, Meghalaya, 
Mizoram, Tripura and Nagaland) for strength ening of the Intra-St ate Transmission and 
Distribution Systems (33kV and above) at an estimated cost of ₹6700 crore. The 
comprehensive scheme for Strengthening of Transmission and Distribution System in 
Arunachal Pradesh and Sikkim at an estimated cost of ₹9129.32 crore has been 
sanctioned.

(c) To coordinate with State Govern ments and local agencies, and to 
monitor the progress of various activities in the North Eastern Region, a dedicated 
monitoring mechanism has been established. Under this framework, Project Quality 
Monitors / Third Party Technical Inspectors (P QM/TPTI) serve as external monitors to 
inspect project implementation. Field-level Technical Support Units provide assistance 
to State Governments in project execution an d inspections. In addition, several review 
meetings are conducted at the level of th e Union Minister with Chief Ministers and 
concerned State Ministers. Regular review meetings are also held by the Secretary and 
senior officers of MDoNER with Chief Se cretaries and senior State officials. 
Furthermore, Central Nodal Officers (CNOs)  and Nodal Officers (NOs) continuously 
monitor the progress of projects in their respective assigned States. 
(d) Focused development is improving conn ectivity, livelihoods, and regional 
integration in the North Eastern Region (N ER) through coordinated policy interventions 
and targeted sectoral initiatives such as. 
i.   AshtalakshmiMahotsav 
AshtalakshmiMahotsav was organized by MDoNER from 6-8th December 2024 with 
an aim to offer a dynamic stage for show casing North east India’s vibrant textile 
sector, tourism opportunities, traditional craftsmanship and distinctive GI tagged 
products. Mahotsav was having  all the 8 NE state Pavilion, Erisilk,Muga silk and GI 
pavilion.  Also B2 G sessions wh erein project proposals of about ₹2500 crore were 
received. Cultural programme and Fashio n show were organised to showcase the 
cultural vibrancy and the vibrant textile sector of NER. 
Memorandum of Understanding (MoUs) were signed between (i) Ministry of 
Development of North Eastern Region (MDoNE R) & Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the 
Kingdom of Thailand and (ii) North East ern Handicrafts and Handloom Development 
Corporation Ltd. (NEHHDC) &Creative Econ omy Agency (CEA) of Government of 
40
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Kingdom of Thailand on 3 rd April, 2025 to institutio nalize and increase bilateral 
cooperation in the handloom and handicraft sector. 
ii. North-East Bankers Conclave 2024 
MDoNER organized a North East Bankers’ Conclave in New Delhi on 28 April 2023 
and the North East Bankers’ Conclave 2. 0 in Agartala, Tripura on 21 December 
2024, during which in-depth deliberations  were held on the current banking 
landscape in the NER with the objective of providin g a roadmap to meet the 
present and future financial resource need s of the region. As a result of these 
initiatives, 131 bank branches and 126 AT Ms were opened in the NER during the 
period from December 2024 to November 2025, and to date, banking services 
have been extended to 6,774 villages etc. 
iii. Rising Northeast Investors Summit 2025: 
 
     Ministry of Development of North Eastern Region (MDoNER) organized "Rising 
North East"-Investors Summit 2025 on 23r d and 24th May, 2025 in New Delhi, 
which was inaugurated by Hon’ble Prime Minister. The Summit was intended to 
promote trade and investment in the Nort h Eastern Region and provide a platform 
for an immersive dialogue between se lect embassies, the business community, 
financial institutions, and relevant gover nment stakeholders for forging mutually 
beneficial partnerships. The event has brou ght about an Investment interest from 
various private investors and PSUs to the tune of ₹ 4.48 lakh crore.  
iv. Model Tourism Circuits 
The Ministry of Development of North East ern Region (MDoNER), in coordination 
with the Governments of the eight North Eastern States, has launched an initiative 
for the development Model Tourist Circuit with the objective of positioning the 
North East as a sustainable, inclusive,  and high-value tourism corridor. The 
initiative is aligned with the national vision of tourism-led economic growth, 
cultural preservation, and enhanced nati onal and global visibility of the North 
Eastern Region. 
S. No. State Model Tourist Circuit 
1. Arunachal Pradesh Itanagar-Chullyu-Ziro

v. USP 
    To embed the North Eastern Region (NER ) firmly within India’s growth discourse, 
the Ministry of Development of North Ea stern Region (MDoNER) is adopting a 
USP-driven branding strategy for all eigh t states. These USPs have been chosen 
after a detailed Comparative and Compet itive analysis of all 8 States vis-à -vis 
each other and vis-a-vis other States and neighbouring countries 
S. No. State USP 
1. Arunachal Pradesh Kiwi 
2. Assam Muga Silk 
3. Manipur Polo 
4. Meghalaya Lakadong Turmeric 
5. Mizoram Ginger 
6. Nagaland Coffee 
7. Sikkim Organic Farming 
8. Tripura Queen Pineapple 
vi. Agarwood: 
It is one of the highest valued non-timber forest product, grows in abundance in 
North East.As per the Botanical Survey of  India in an assessment made in 2024, 
north east statesaccount for about 96.6% of India’s agarwood tree base in India. 
Due to its high export potential, Government on the basis of its recommendationof 
an Inter-Ministerial Task Force in 2023approved a roadmap in 2024to guide 
sustainable cultivation, processing and trade. The NE States have been advised 
to prepare area-expansion plans, supported by geospatial mapping in Tripura and 
Assam, while export promotion has been  significantly strengthened through 
enhancement of annual export quotas under CITES Convention to 1,51,080 kg for 
chips and 7,050 kg for oil. To streamline exports, processing of application for 
export of agarwood products has been integrated on the DGFT portal. GI tagging 
42
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
has also been taken up by North Ea stern Region Agricultural Marketing 
Corporation (NERAMAC) Ltd in collabora tion with NABARD.Proof of Concept 
(PoC) has also been approved for development of two Agarwood clusters at 
Kadamtala (Tripura) and Golaghat (Assam) .Collectively, these interventions aim 
to expand cultivation, strengthen value chains, and boost formal, traceable 
agarwood exports from the North Eastern Region. 
vii. Bamboo:  
A total 25 projects worth ₹236.53 crore for strengthenin g bamboo sector in NER 
have been sanctioned by NEC. North East Cane and Bamboo Development 
Council (NECBDC) has trained a total of  5068 bamboo artisans and officials 
during the last three years. Ministry of DoNER has sanctioned two Projects with a 
total outlay of         ₹ 82.50 crore for strengthening of traditional bamboo artisan 
clusters by enhancing market access, modernizing products, and linking them 
with digital and retail ecosystem; as we ll as for promotion of engineered bamboo 
products through cluster strengthening and export facilitation in NER. 
viii. High Level Task Force 
Focused development is improving conn ectivity, livelihoods, and regional 
integration in the North Eastern Regi on (NER) through coordinated policy 
interventions and targeted sectoral init iatives. The Ministry of Development of 
North Eastern Region (MDoNER) has constituted eight High-Level Task Forces 
(HLTFs) for accelerating sectoral growth in the region, each chaired by a Chief 
Minister of a North Eastern State, with  the Union Minister (MDoNER) and three 
other Chief Ministers as members. These Task Forces have been formed on the 
subject such as, (1) Promotion of Sports in NER, (2) North East Economic 
Corridor, (3) Investment Promotion in NER, (4) Handlooms and Handicrafts in 
NER, (5) Tourism in NER, (6) Infrastr ucture and Connectivity in NER, (7) 
Agriculture & Horticulture for addressing  gaps in value chain and market, and 
(8) Attaining Self-reliance in milk, eggs, fish and meat production in NER.

Role of Bharatgen AI 
*63.   SHRI BHUBANESWAR KALITA: Will  the Minister of SCIENCE AND 
TECHNOLOGY be pleased to state: 
(a)  the number of Indian languages wh ich are currently supported by Bharat 
Gen's AI models and the roadmap for full language coverage; 
(b)  the specific applications of Bharat  Gen in the healthcare, agriculture, 
education and governance sectors; 
(c) the number of Technology Innovati on Hubs and Translational Research 
Parks currently active under the Bharat Gen network; and 
(d)  the institutions which are part of th e Bharat Gen consortium and what role it 
plays in its development? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE OF THE MINISTRY OF SCIENCE AND  
TECHNOLOGY (DR. JITENDRA SINGH): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the table of 
the House. 
                                                              Statement  
(a) to (d) BharatGen is the first governm ent supported national initiative to 
develop a range of sovereign foundational AI  models tailored to Indian languages and 
societal contexts. It spans multiple modalit ies, including text (via Large Language 
Models), speech (Text-to-Speech and Automatic Speech Recognition), and vision-
language systems. 
Currently, BharatGen’s AI models support  15 Indian languages which include 
Hindi, Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Kannada , Maithili, Malayalam, Marathi, Nepali, 
Oriya, Punjabi, Sanskrit, Sindhi, Tamil and Telugu. Soon, all 22 scheduled Indian 
languages will be covered. 
BharatGen has released domain speci fic fine-tuned models for Ayurveda 
(AyurParam), Indian agriculture (AgriParam) and Indian legal domain (Legal Param). 
In addition, all BharatGen models ( text, sp eech and vision) are useful for applications 
across healthcare, agriculture, education and governance.  
Two Technology Innovation Hubs namely  TIH Foundation for IoT and IoE, IIT 
Bombay and IITM Pravartak Technologies Foundation, IIT Madras under National 
Mission on Interdisciplinary Cyber-Physical Systems (NM-ICPS) of Department of 
Science and Technology (DST) are currently active as part of BharatGen network. 
48
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
The following institutions are a part of the BharatGen consortium:

SHRI BHUBANESWAR KALITA: Mr. Chairman, Sir,  the mention of Artificial Intelligence 
has become a global phenomenon covering mu ltiple sectors of human life.  Under the 
leadership of hon. Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, the BharatGen is the first 
Government supported national initiative to  develop a range of sovereign foundational 
AI models tailored to Indian languages and social context.  This is one sector.  I want to 
know from the hon. Minister whether the Gove rnment is planning to expand it to other 
sectors of human life.   
DR. JITENDRA SINGH:  Mr. Chairman, Sir, I must compliment the hon. Member for 
having asked a very significant question, which is very relevant to the contemporary 
[ 05 February, 2026 ]
49

 
 
times and has futuristic bearing also.  We would recall that hon. Prime Minister, Modi ji, 
had launched India AI Mission in March, 2024 and this BharatGen, which the hon. 
Member is referring to, was also an outc ome of the Mission. The peculiarity or the 
special feature of this is that this is a Government-owned sovereign AI model of large 
language.  This is first of its kind which is  owned by the Government.  It is sovereign 
and it is tailored to the Indian languages and the Indian societal text.  There are very few 
models like this across the world.  If you s ee, in Asia also, we only have about six 
countries - China, Japan, Thailand, Singap ore and Malaysia, who have large language 
model but that too is not the same as ours for the simple reason that they are much 
more homogenous societies and the languages spoken in those nations are just one or 
two while we have 22 languages in the Eighth Schedule.   
 With regard to the hon. Member’s ques tion regarding its expansion, we have 
already completed 15 languages.  I think, w e  w i l l  b e  c o m p l e t i n g  t h e  r e s t  o f  t h e  
languages, and we will complete all the 22 la nguages in this month itself.  This has 
three modules – the text, the speech and the document-vision, and, then, of course, 
there are dedicated domains like Ayurvedic, Ag ri and Legal system.  All the 22 will have 
text module completed by this month and 15 will have the speech and vision modules 
completed as well.  Therefore, it is going  to be a huge game changer for all the 
stakeholders.                                                     
SHRI BHUBANESWAR KALITA: Thank you, Sir, for giving me the opportunity to ask the

DR. JITENDRA SINGH :  Sir, the hon. Member has rightly mentioned it.  We are 
following a very integrated approach, whole of science, whole of Government, whole of 
nation. So, as he rightly mentioned, the Bh aratGen initiative has been undertaken or 
spearheaded by IIT, Bombay.  But we have se ven other institutions as a part of the 
consortium which include IIT Hyderabad, IIT Madras, IIT Kanpur, IIT Mandi, IIT Indore 
and so on.  But that does not mean that we have left out other regions.  In fact, we 
have come up with 25 technology innovation hubs, out of which four have already been 
50
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
upgraded to the level of the Technology Transfer Search Parks where you have the 
industry coexisting along with the research departments and, therefore, the transfer 
becomes much, much easier, convenient an d facilitated. These four upgraded hubs 
are IIT Indore, IIT Kanpur, IIT Dhanbad and In dian Institute of Sciences in Bengaluru. 
The rest are also living up to the same ma ndate of robotics, AI, ML, cyber security.  
Gradually, we are expanding ou t. But when we come to the languages part of the 
BharatGen module as such, the languages of the North East, which are a part of the 22 
languages included in the Eighth Schedule, will also be included. Rather, I would add 
that since this is a dynamic process, some times, we may require more than 22 also. 
So, that is precisely what I was suggesting with the team and discussing with them.  
After we have completed the 22 languages, we may go further also. For example, in 
Uttar Pradesh, you have three-four dialects, even for Hindi.  So, to begin with, we take 
Hindi because when the AI model speaks, it speaks in a different dialect and the 
translation or the conversion of it to anothe r language or another vi sion or another text 
sometimes becomes more appropriate.  So, we are in a dynamic expansionist mode 
and we might go even beyond 22 languages and beyond these regions. So, certainly, 
North East would be a very much a definite part because North East already has been a 
high priority for Prime Minister Modi.  Rather, this gives me an opportunity to say that 
Modiji, as Prime Minister, has travelled al most 70 times to the North East, which is 
more than the number of times put together  by all the preceding Prime Ministers have 
travelled to North East. 
SHRI BHUBANESWAR KALITA: Sir, my specific question is whether IIT Guwahati can

DR. JITENDRA SINGH:  Yes, certainly.  We have not ended this process. For the time

crossing mode because this is a part of a larger ecosystem, which also includes the 
Anusandhan National Research  Foundation. So, they are al ready into this, to include 
even those institutions, and it was one of th e motives behind having National Research 
Foundation because sometimes there was an input or a grievance from certain quarters 
that some of the first line institutions got a better treatment because of the better 
infrastructure. So, in order to democratize that, we already have put an infrastructure in 
place. That also, this Government did. And cer tainly, IIT Guwahati would also be a part 
of it.

Ǜी सभापित:  क्वेÌचन नÇबर 64. 
Veer Parivar Sahayata Yojna 
#*64.  SHRI  BRIJ LA:  Will the Minister of LAW AND JUSTICE be pleased to state: 
(a) the objectives and scope of NALSA Veer Parivar Sahayata Yojna, 2025; 
(b) the number of legal service centres proposed or established at the State and 
district Sainik Welfare Board levels along with the status of their coverage in 
the States/Union territories; 
(c) the extent to which digital platforms, e-services, and priority-based 
grievance redressal mechanisms are being utilized under the scheme and 
the details of Inter-State coordination mechanism, particularly in the cases 
involving Multi-State jurisdictions; and 
(d) the details of monitoring, reporting and capacity-building measures adopted 
to ensure effective implementation and widespread reach of the scheme? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE OF THE MINISTRY OF LAW AND JUSTICE (SHRI ARJUN 
RAM MEGHWAL): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
                                                            Statement  
(a)  The Veer Parivar Sahayata Yojana (VPSY), 2025, launched by the National Legal 
Services Authority (NALSA), aims to provid e accessible and effective legal services to 
serving defence personnel, ex-servicemen and their dependent family members who 
are eligible under the Legal Se rvices Authorities Act, 1987, in matters such as property 
disputes, welfare schemes, matrimonial disputes, civil disputes, and other legal 
matters.The Scheme is being implemented through Legal Services Clinics, particularly 
established at Rajya and Zila Sainik Boards in  coordination with State and District Legal 
Services Authorities. 
                                                            
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
 
 
54
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
(b)  As on 31.12.2025, there are 31 Legal Services Clinics at Rajya Sainik Boards and 
417 Legal Service Clinics at Zila Sainik Boards.   
(c) Under the provision of Clause 8of the Sc heme, digital platforms and e-Services 
of NALSA are used to ensure seamless acce ssibility and efficiency  in providing legal 
services or assistance to the beneficiarie s. The Para-Legal Volunteers (PLVs) and 
Panel Lawyers of the Legal Services Clinics at the Zila and Rajya Sainik Boards: - 
(i)   Identify and refer eligible cases , through SLSA/DLSA/TLSC, particularly 
family and matrimonial disputes, for st ructured online mediation by trained 
mediators empanelled by DLSAs.  
(ii)   Suitable civil, pension, property, or family-related cases are being identified 
and in districts with a high number of  defence-related cases, dedicated slots 
or virtual rooms are being organi sed for defence personnel and ex-
servicemen as part of the routine e-Lok Adalat framework. 
(iii)   Guide beneficiaries in applying for legal services or assistance through the 
online application portal/module-based app, where applicants can track their 
case status and access legal information. 
(iv)   Conduct awareness sessions to edu cate defence families on how to access 
and use digital legal assistance platforms. 
The Member Secretary, SLSA act as a referral hub for Inter-State grievances, 
forwarding cases to other SLSAs where applicable.  The Inter-Stat e referral data is 
maintained as per the Reporting Format specified in the Scheme. 
(d) The implementation of the Scheme is  monitored by NALS A through reports 
received from SLSAs as per the prescribed  format.  A structured coordination 
committee mechanism has been established at the National, State and District levels to 
ensure effective implementation and monitori ng of welfare measures for the families of 
martyrs and ex-servicemen.  This multi-tier  coordination mechanism is intended to 
ensure timely delivery of assistance and effective inter-departmental coordination.  As 
on 31.12.2025, 01 National, 12 State and 269 District Level coordination committees are 
functional.  
Capacity-building is undertaken through regular training and sensitisation 
programmes for legal aid lawyers, Para-Legal  Volunteers to ensure effective outreach 
and uniform implementation across States. As on 31.12.025, 310 training programmes 
[ 05 February, 2026 ]
55

 
 
and 2141 awareness camps have been initia ted by various clinics. As part of 77 th 
Republic Day, SLSAs have undertaken focused awareness, outreach and grievance 
resolution drive under the Scheme.

Conference Clearance System 
#*65. SHRI MADAN RATHORE: Will the Minister  of EXTERNAL AFFAIRS be pleased to 
state: 
                                                            
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
[ 05 February, 2026 ]
59

 
 
(a) the number of applications received through the Conference Clearance 
System portal in the last three year s, including the details of approved, 
rejected, and withdrawn applications; 
(b)  the average processing time for gran ting political clearance before and after 
the launch of online portal; 
(c)   whether any time limits or service standards have been set for the disposal of 
applications, and the extent to which they have been complied with; and 
(d)     if so, the details thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF EXTERNAL AFFAIRS (SHRI KIRTI 
VARDHAN SINGH):  (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the Table of the House.  
                                                                Statement  
(a) to (d) The Conference Cl earance System (CCS) portal 
(conference.epolclearance.gov.in) is an  automated, single -window web- based 
application developed by the Ministry of External Affairs in March 2025 to streamline the 
process for granting political clearance to foreign nationals to attend or participate in 
conferences/seminars/workshops/ sports events and other events organised in India 
by government or private bodies.  
 This digital platform has ensured a smoother and hassle-free user experience 
with a substantial reduction in processing ti me as compared to the earlier mechanism. 
The portal also serves the la rger objective of Digital Indi a by maximizing the number of 
services available on the digital platform. 
 The portal serves as a cen tralised platform where stakeholders can access all 
the information related to the status of an  application in one place. It features an 
interactive dashboard that enables the Ministry to seek missing or additional information 
from organisers promptly, whenever required. Organisers can also monitor the status 
of their applications online. These features have substantially reduced the average 
processing time by at least five to six working days.  
 In general, political clearance is gran ted within 3-5 working days. However, the 
actual processing time may vary on a case-to -case basis depending on several factors 
including the number of invitees – which ma y range from 10 to 200 and the countries 
                                                                                                                                                                                            
 
 
60
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
from which participation is expected. The po rtal also includes a provision for partial 
clearance. Under this m echanism, political clearance is gr anted individually as soon as 
complete information for a foreign delegate is received and verifi ed. As a result, the 
overall application status may continue to appear as “pending” even though the 
political clearance has been granted for a majo rity of the delegates in some cases upto 
90 percent of the total list.  
 A total number of 4809 applications have been received on the conference 
clearance portal since its launch. The details of these applications are as under:-

MR. CHAIRMAN:  First supplementary, Shri Madan Rathore. 
Ǜी मदन राठौड़: सभापित महोदय, मȅ आपके  माध्यम से माननीय मंतर्ी जी से जानना चाहता हूँ िक 
Conference e-Political Clearance System क्या है और Conference e-Political Clearance 
System क े िलए कौन आवेदन कर सकता है? 
SHRI KIRTIVARDHAN SINGH:  Sir, the Conference e-Political Clearance System is 
another public service delivery initiative taken by  the Ministry of Extern al Affairs. This is 
under the visionary leadership of Prime Mini ster, Shri Narendra Modi, where the focus 
is on modernizing consular and citizen-centric services that can be provided by the 
Ministry. This Conference Clearance System is a single-window portal and it is an 
automated web-based application developed by our Ministry in March 2025.  The 
objective is to streamline the process for granting political clearance to foreign nationals 
[ 05 February, 2026 ]
61

 
 
to attend or participate in conferences, se minars, workshops, sports events, or other 
events which are organized in our country by either our Government or even by private 
bodies.  As far as the question as to who all can participate in this, Sir, anybody who is 
holding a conference here in India, any orga nization, any educational institute, which 
have invited foreign participants, they have to apply on an online portal. The 
organization has to apply on an online portal  and the details of all the participants are 
given and then all the information is sought  from our Coordination Committee, from our 
territorial offices and also from our Missions abroad, and based on that information, 
clearance is given to the participants and to the organizers.

BRS Party, my leader, KCRji, my colleagues and myself, I thank the hon. President for 
her Address.  While running through the Address, we noticed some commendable 
issues which were mentioned in the Rashtrapat iji’s Address, like lifting 24 crore citizens 
out of poverty, Jal Jeevan Mission,  piped water to 11 crore families -- of course, this is 
the Telangana model, Mission Bhagiratha  -- and also becoming the fifth largest 
economy. Now these are commendable achieve ments, Mr. Deputy Chairman, Sir.  
But, out of the 56 paragraphs of Rashtrapat iji’s Address, paragraph 11 is what really 
intrigued me.  In paragraph 11, the Government spoke about express reforms. They 
[ 05 February, 2026 ]
85

 
 
spoke about reforms in GST and reforms in IT. Well, they are commendable. Reforms 
in GST are helping small, marginal farmers and poor people in buying daily 
commodities. Reducing taxes would, definite ly, create some savings. IT reforms have 
been very helpful for the middle class. We  appreciate that.  These are all revenue 
generations for the Government. But, today,  as most Members while speaking on the 
Address felt, there is major concern about the growing inequalities in India.  While India 
continues to grow, inequality is an area of concern.  Of course, I would not like to dwell 
too much on the figures, but it is a matter of  concern.  And I, in my personal view, can 
point out one fundamental reason for this growing inequality.   
Sir, I would like to emphasize on two is sues in my speech on the Motion of 
Thanks -- one is land and the other is wate r -- and how this inequality is growing by 
the year. Fifty per cent of India's workforce relies on agriculture. When 50 per cent of 
India is engaged in agriculture or allied activities, what is it that they are contributing? 
When you look at their contribution to the GDP, while services contribute more than 55 
per cent, industry contributes almost 27 per cent and agriculture contributes close to 18 
per cent. So, with 50 per cent people working on agriculture and contributing only 18 
per cent to GDP, and with agriculture growing at just four per cent, surely inequalities 
would continue to grow in this country. Of course, to address this issue, right from 
Nehru ji to Modi ji, they have emphasized it.  We very profoundly look back into Pandit 
Nehru ji's statement, where he said, “Major irrigation dams ar e the temples of modern 
India.”  And then, we look at recent Modi ji's statement, where he very categorically 
reassured the nation, especially the farmers, that Kisan, at any cost, will be protected. 
Well, these are very good and very encouraging words. Now, today, if I speak about 50 
per cent of India's workforce, people who are not dependent on agriculture, right from 
the Supreme Court lawyers to the IT professi onals to anyone, they have a concern for 
the farmer in the village. Th ey have a concern for his pr osperity. But, unfortunately, 
having the world's most arable land and almost 70,000 TMC of God-given water, India, 
today, allows 50 per cent of this resource of water to flow untapped into the sea.  Now, 
this is at  this place, as outlined in pa ragraph 11 regarding express reforms mentioned 
by the Government, I think, the Government 's move should come in. All the major 
rivers in the country, today, are under inte r-State dispute. Be it Krishna, Godavari, 
Cauvery, Ravi, Bias, Narmada, you know, a ll the rivers are under dispute. Now, to 
overcome that, we look back and we see the nine Tribunals have been constituted so 
far in the country today. I believe six are f unctioning, but there is no respite, no 
solution. Beyond Tribunals, specific Authorit ies have been created , be it the Cauvery 
Water Management Authority, the Bhakra -Bias Management Board, the Narmada 
86
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Control Authority and the River Boards Act. These initiatives have been constituted for 
the last 70 years, but nothing works. This is where the political will has to come in, this 
is where paragraph 11 should come in and this is where the hon. Prime Minister should 
intervene. Earlier mechanisms were there. Fundamentally, to achieve this, in spite of 
having Tribunals/Boards, it is the political convergence which, in my belief, can find a 
solution to it.  A political convergence was possible earlier when you had the NDC, the 
National Development Council, which, unfortuna tely, last met in 201 2. After that, NITI 
Aayog came in and that, I presume, has falle n sick. But NITI Aayog has, definitely, 
come up with ideas but to what level is NITI Aayog implementing its ideas is the 
concern. Unfortunately, it is powerless.  Then, we had the Inter-State Council, which is 
supposed to meet thrice a year.  I believe, the last meeting was held in 2016 to discuss 
the Punchhi Commission Report and, fundam entally, the agenda on that day was to 
discuss federalism and inter-State water link ing. But, unfortunately, that being the 
subject, that happened to be the last meeting also of the Inter-State Council. There 
was an assurance given, I believe, that In ter-State River Dispute (Amendment) Bill, 
which was there, will be taken up, amended and all the aspects discussed in the 
Commission could probably be incorporated there.  The Interstate River Dispute 
Management Bill did come up. What happene d subsequently was,  in spite of its 
discussion in Lok Sabha, it could not see th e light of the day. The Bill lapsed and today 
we are back to square one.  So, this is where I feel that Modi ji's Government, the 
express reforms, need to come in here. Empathy is needed. Solutions have to be 
found because these are the issues.  Today , unless the river waters are settled, the 
pace of progress that you are looking for, in  our view, may not be achieved, given that 
India's 50 per cent of people are dependent on water for agriculture and other means. If 
the inter-State river dispute is  not solved, at least, optimi se the water usage. Till you 
find a solution to that, you should optimise the available resources.  Our record states 
that one TMC of water can irri gate 10,000 acres of land.  In  my State, during KCR Ji’s 
time, we had experimented.  We had ensured that even within the available resources, 
with one TMC of water, we could irrigate 16,000 acres of land.  So, 16,000 acres of land 
was irrigated with what we call the ‘one and off’ system, where irrigation was given to a 
canal for two days, stopped and then again given.  This not only ensured that the entire 
State got covered, but it also ensured that more productivity came in.  So, we saved 
water and we got more productivity.  An d, of course, I would like to compliment 
Shivrajji -- he is not here -- on the Mohanpura Lift Irrigation Project, which they 
initiated.  One TMC went up to 18,000 acres .  That was through the piped irrigation 
scheme, which Madhya Pradesh Government  started.  The hon. Prime Minister 
inaugurated it.  But, then, unfortunately, it got limited only to that project.  If it was

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Dr. Sarfraz Ahmad; not present.  Shrimati Sudha Murty. 
SHRIMATI SUDHA MURTY (Nominated): Than k you, Mr. Deputy Chairman, Sir, for 
giving me an opportunity to speak. I just want to quote one line from the President’s 
Address, “The nation is working on every front to preserve and enrich our culture and 
heritage.” Now, taking the cue from there, I wa nt to speak about our country's culture 
and heritage.   
Around 2,000 years back, they started a ro ad from Xi'an to Istanbul, Istanbul to 
Rome, which is popularly called the Silk Road . It was just not a road; it also carried 
ideas, religion, business and many more things . Globally, a country's culture is defined 
b y i t s  ro ad s  o n  t h e  l a n d .   B u t ,  t o d ay,  I  want to speak about something which is our 
national heritage, which is our culture and which we have, to some extent, neglected. 
That is ‘Project Mausam’. What is it? You look at India's tip in southern India. It is 
connected to an Ocean which is known as the Hind Mahasagar or the Indian Ocean. In 
olden days, it had a great influence on 39 co untries – from  the eastern coast of South 
Africa to Zanzibar and Aden, across  the  African continent to present-day’s UAE, 
Pakistan and India, and eastward to Burma, Bhutan, Philippines, Vietnam and many 
other independent islands. There was a rout e; there was a culture. We, Indians, are 
not colony makers.  We have  never colonised anybody by power, but we did influence 
people by our ideas, by our behaviour, by our honesty and by our business. Hence, all 
these 39 countries, which I am talking, in Hind Mahasagar , were influenced by India 
and so many trade routes were  developed in that, but we have neglected that in our 
Indian history, in our culture, which is a gr eat heritage to our India. All these countries 
[ 05 February, 2026 ]
107

 
 
have one or other effect of India's culture. Many names in that entire South Asia had 
Indian names like Panduranga, Javadweepa, Suvarnadweepa.  Over time, we call 
Panduranga as Padang, Javaddweepa as Java and Suvarnadweepa as Sumatra.  
Now, I want to request to take this project forward. In monsoon winds, the sailors, 
from time immemorial, in olden days, used to sail from all these countries via India, 
then go to the Southeast countries.  After the monsoon season was over, the reverse is 
to happen. The sailors will sail from other end back to their country.  The best example 
you can see in Odisha. They call it Bali Yatra. That means, after Sharad Purnima  is 
over, people used to travel and the best example can be seen near Konark. Once in a 
year, after that festival, they leave the lamp s and elders in the fa mily will come to the 
seashore, indicating our merchants used to travel to the far off place.  Even today, they 
are celebrating that, as my colleague, Dr. Sa smit Patra, told me. This shows India's 
great history, great culture and great connect ivity.  What Madam President has told is 
that we should preserve it. In 2014, I supp ose, a proposal was made called the Project 
Mausam because it is the monsoon winds which can connect this end to that end.   I 
would request our Government to take this initiative taking hon. President Madam’s 
cue and under able leadership of our hon. Prime Minister Modiji, who believes in India’s 
culture, our Sanskriti, the greatness of Mo ther India, our Bharat, to take Mission 
Mausam global, particularly in Hind Mahasa gar and see in all thes e places, what effect 
we can have there.  There are caves near Eden where yo u have Buddhist sculptures 
and Buddhist stupas are there, indicating that our merchants went up to that point.   
 I really would like to thank our hon. Pr ime Minister Modiji for giving me this 
opportunity, making me a Rajya Sabha Member  by nominating me, giving me a great 
canvas to talk, express my country’s legacy, my country’s cultural heritage, my 
country’s prosperity in every aspect of life, which we live and, with positive attitude, we 
should go ahead with such projects.  We should  make it very familiar at the global level 
indicating that our country has a fantastic history, fantastic culture and it is aadi, 
ananta, apoorva, and thank you once again, Sir, for giving me an opportunity to talk 
about Mission Mausam which contains 39 countries, South East Asia, independent 
islands, India in the middle and then Unit ed Arab Emirates, Africa and all those 
countries.  Dhanyavad; Jai Hind; Vande Mataram!
